NTA. It’s absolutely ridiculous what he’s been doing. I would suggest looking over your lease. It also doesn’t matter what he says or shows up doing. He’s completely in the wrong for what he’s doing. It seems like he thinks he can do what he wants because you’re young…..just my opinion.

NTA. Your landlord is being sketchy as hell. Sending blanket emails without specifics, showing up unannounced, walking into bedrooms without permission (especially while someone is SLEEPING?!), and getting angry when you lock your doors? That's not "covering his ass," that's him being controlling and violating your privacy. You have EVERY RIGHT to ask for 24-48 hours notice before he enters your apartment. That's not unreasonable—that's literally tenant law in most places. The fact that he's getting angry about locked bedroom doors tells me he KNOWS what he's doing isn't okay. Good call on having a family friend who's in law review your lease. I have a feeling your landlord is overstepping his legal authority here. Most jurisdictions require landlords to give proper notice before entering a rental unit, and "surprise showings" without telling you which apartment or when is NOT proper notice. Stand your ground on this one. Don't let your roommates convince you that you're being difficult. You're protecting your privacy and your rights as a tenant. If he wants to show your apartment, he can give you a heads up like a normal person. 🚩

Item 3 · target user_686

stance NTAwarrant autonomy_boundariesdonor's warrant care_harmhistory 5913 / 5748 words
the post (577 words)
This is a long story, but I will try to keep it short. About a year and a half ago, my (32M) wife's (36F) best friend Sadie was dumped by her long-term boyfriend of ten years. He said that he had fallen out of love and came home one night and just said it was over. She took it hard, as anyone would, and for the next year or so, my wife and I took on the primary emotional support of the friend group--inviting her over for dinner, letting her stay with us, helping her move out of the house they shared, etc. During that time, she wasn't getting over the breakup, but she was steadily moving in the right direction--going to a therapist and working through it. She accused him of cheating, but that wasn't really substantiated. About six months into the breakup, she was staying with another friend Pam, who was going through her own personal stuff that we didn't know about, namely, a divorce that she had yet to make public. One night, Pam said to Sadie that because her boyfriend didn't cheat on her or abuse her, she just needed to get over it and it wasn't a big deal. Of course, we thought this was a callous, stupid, and insensitive thing to say. However, in the aftermath, Sadie basically decided to cut off that entire side of the friend group. We spent a lot of time trying to get them to reconcile, trying to get Pam to apologize, but nothing worked. We understood Sadie cutting off the friend group, however, we are friends with many people in Pam's orbit, and were somewhat unwilling to cut those people off as we had just moved o the city we now live in and enjoy the company of a lot of people who Pam is friends with, making it somewhat unavoidable to cut Pam completely out of our life. We also felt empathy for Pam, who said a stupid thing, but was also hurting secretly. About six months ago we were invited to a party at Pam's house and decided to go. We knew it would hurt Sadie, so we got out ahead of it, and my wife had a conversation with her about how we were going to go, and that we hoped she wouldn't take this as us endorsing what Pam said. We wanted to go out and have a fun night with friends. After this, Sadie has cut us out of her life. My wife still invites her over and is pretty heartbroken about the whole thing, as they have been very close friends for a long time. I want to have empathy for the situation, but I am sort of in the camp that we have done a lot for Sadie, and that not being able to forgive this and throwing away 15 years of friendship is extreme on her part. My wife is torn up about it and has been taking the whole thing pretty hard. She even went over to apologize and was told by Sadie that she wasn't standing up for her (even after all her efforts to mend the relationship between Pam and Sadie). So basically, AITA for going to this party against Sadie's wishes, and do we owe her an apology or were we in the right not to cut off an entire part of our friend group because of this disagreement between Sadie and Pam?
You're allowed to have multiple friends and multiple friend groups. Sadie is being unreasonable and childish with her "it's me or her" attitude over a single remark. Stop trying to force a reconcilation between them. Tell them both something like "Your relationship with \[whoever\] is your business, but we're not going to ignore them"...and leave it at that. NTA.
same: the target's own historyjudge 0.72 · embedding 0.72 · read as NTA / autonomy_boundaries
NTA - but your wife needs to stop trying to manage this situation. You and your wife were in a tough spot, and you handled it reasonably by giving Sadie a heads-up instead of sneaking around. You weren't obligated to cut off an entire friend group because Sadie and Pam had a falling out, especially when you'd already spent considerable time and energy trying to get them to reconcile. Here's the thing though: Sadie is clearly still hurting, and she's lashing out. That's understandable, but it doesn't make it your responsibility to fix. You can't force someone to forgive you or to accept your choices, no matter how reasonable those choices are. Your wife going over to apologize when you didn't actually do anything wrong is just feeding into this dynamic where Sadie gets to dictate your social life. The hard truth is that sometimes friendships end, even long ones. It sucks. But Sadie made a choice to cut you off because you wouldn't choose her over everyone else in your life - which, frankly, is an unreasonable ask. You were supportive during her breakup. You tried to mediate. You were honest with her about your plans. That's what good friends do. Your wife needs to grieve this friendship and then let it go. Continuing to reach out and apologize for something you didn't do wrong is just prolonging the pain for both of them. Sometimes you have to accept that someone isn't going to forgive you, even when you don't owe them an apology.
judge's key points and reasoning
Key Point 1: The poster is not the asshole (NTA) - they were reasonable in attending the party and were not obligated to cut off an entire friend group. Item 12 · target user_469

stance NTAwarrant property_consentdonor's warrant tradition_expectationshistory 5939 / 5779 words
the post (303 words)
We live in Australia where we have “op shops” which are effectively thrift stores run for charities by volunteers, mainly retired older women. (I don’t know if it’s the same elsewhere in the world, but I’m trying to paint the picture). They vary in quality - some are very well organised and clean, some a very junky with soiled goods and bits of rubbish. I visited an op shop I hadn’t been to before on the weekend with my 6 year old, and my friend and her 6 year old. The kids were checking out the toy section, which were a few sparse shelves with mostly plastic baby toys, and some buckets with smaller bits thrown in them. They were looking around and picking things up to look at them while I was browsing. My friend’s son pressed some buttons on a toy and it sent off sounds and flashing lights on the toy. That’s when a woman who worked there came over and was really quite unpleasant - she said “Don’t let the children play with the toys. They aren’t for playing.” I was surprised and didn’t know what to say - I mean, you can press the buttons on toys in Target, and the kids weren’t being rough or anything. She seemed annoyed at my dumbfounded silence and gruffly said “They’re for sale, stop them playing”. I think I frowned before turning away and ignoring her, as my instinct was to disagree but didn’t want to be rude with the kids there (so I was rude in a quieter way). Is this a thing? AITA? I know it’s a small thing but I’m surprised by her insistence that it was the wrong thing to do, and felt bad for the kids when I’d said it was ok to touch the toys.
NTA. If this were a toy store where every toy is brand new, the worker might have had a point. Toy stores don't want you treating them as convenient playgrounds while you shop. They don't want your kids ripping open packages to try everything out. (They often have demonstration models on display for precisely that reason.) If you buy something from a toy store, you can take it for granted that the toy does work as promised, and if it doesn't, then at least you have some recourse - you can return the toy for a refund or replacement. But this isn't a toy store. It's an op shop, and different rules apply. The toys are all second hand. They might not work properly. They might not work at all! The innards might be broken, or there might be a vital piece missing. If you buy a second hand toy without trying it out, and you take it home and discover it doesn't work, I very much doubt the op shop would give you your money back. For that reason, it is perfectly reasonable to try out a second hand toy - or more importantly, let your kids try it out - before deciding whether to buy it. And for some toys, "trying it out" means it's going to make a racket. All you were required to do was make sure the kids didn't get in anyone else's way, didn't leave the toy area messy, and didn't damage the toys. As long as you did that, you were being a responsible parent and you were not the asshole. The woman was unreasonable. Fortunately, there are plenty of op shops around. Avoid that one and shop at another.

NTA. The volunteer was being unreasonable and frankly, rude. Kids exploring toys in a thrift store by pressing buttons or picking them up to examine them is completely normal and expected behavior. Your comparison to Target is spot on. In any retail environment, customers - including children - are expected to interact with products to some degree. That's how shopping works. If the op shop didn't want children touching the toys, they should have put them behind glass or in a locked cabinet, not left them on open shelves where kids can reach them. The volunteer's attitude was also unnecessarily hostile. She could have gently redirected the children if she was concerned, but instead she was gruff and made the kids feel bad for doing something perfectly reasonable. That's not good customer service, charity shop or otherwise. You did the right thing by not engaging with her rudeness in front of the kids. But you also did the right thing by letting the kids know it was okay to touch the toys - because it was. Don't feel bad about that. The volunteer was the problem here, not you or the children.

NTA on your decision. Your mom was an AH, but I'm not totally sure how much of an AH she was/ is. Any decisions about YOUR wedding dress are yours alone to make. Period. Bonus info is the fact that you have been supportive and completely unconcerned with cousin copying so much from your wedding. (Wise and mature on your part!) No one can claim that you are or have been gate-keeping in any way. How nice that cousin likes your dress. No surprise, since she liked pretty much everything about your wedding. That does not translate into her having any claim on your dress. I don't think your mom was an AH to raise the idea - as long as she accepted your answer. The reason I'm unsure about whether your mom is/was an AH is how you described your reaction. Mom raised the idea, and (according to you) you flipped out. It's clear that mom asked "Can't you just be happy for her?" - which IS her not accepting your answer. That does make her an AH. If mom (or cousin) has continued to push the idea, then she/they are clearly full-on AHs. But your post doesn't make that clear. If it was just that one sentence from your mom, then I think you can (1) remain firm in your decision and (2) take a breathe - if it seems they have accepted your decision. It may (legitimately) bug you that your mom prioritized cousin's feelings over yours; but IF mom backed off, then please also give her credit for that. A lot of folks in these posts don't do that.

NTA I think the real issue for you to think about, because I'm sure that it will come up again, is that she is judging what you get for her against what you get for other people. Now you might not think this way, but some people DO consider certain items as 'better' than other items of the same type. In this case, she considers gold to be the top. So if you give ANYONE any gold item, she wants to be one of the people you give it to, if not the only person you give it to. The extra cost of white gold is the penalty she wants you to pay for not getting her gold in the first place. This gold vs silver thing isn't the only potential comparison. If you treat a friend to lunch, she will compare that place to the places you take her for lunch. Just be aware that this is how she thinks. If you want to avoid these kinds of arguments then you need to make sure that you never give anyone anything that ranks higher in desirability than what you give her. It goes without saying that the trick will be for you to figure out how she ranks things. For example, you thought the aesthetic was the standard but it wasn't. In her mind, gold outranks aesthetics when metals are involved.

same: the target's own historyjudge 0.17 · embedding 0.56 · read as NTA / role_obligation
NTA You have every right to set boundaries around gift giving, especially when it makes you uncomfortable. However, your girlfriend comes from a completely different background where gift giving is a love language and a major family tradition. This isn't just about the money to her—it's about how she shows love and how her family shows love. Here's the thing: you two need to find a compromise that works for BOTH of you. She needs to respect that receiving expensive gifts triggers uncomfortable feelings from your childhood, and you need to understand that refusing gifts entirely might feel like rejection to her. Some options to consider: 1 Ask her to give you experiences instead of things—concert tickets, a nice dinner, a trip somewhere. Experiences don't sit around making you feel guilty. 2 Set a reasonable budget cap that you're comfortable with—maybe $50 or $75—and ask her to stick to that. 3 Ask for gifts that are consumable or practical but nice—good quality coffee, a nice bottle of wine, a massage gift certificate. 4 Let her give you ONE gift per year instead of multiple holidays. The key is having a calm conversation where you BOTH feel heard. She's not wrong for wanting to express love through gifts, and you're not wrong for being uncomfortable with it. But "I don't want anything" puts her in an impossible position and makes her feel rejected. Work together on this.

Last year, I (26M, based in Sydney) borrowed my housemate’s car. I was on my P1 license and unfortunately got caught speeding. That came with a $140 fine and 1 demerit point (P1 drivers only get 4 before they lose their license). When the notice arrived, my housemate (who has a full license with a much higher point threshold) offered to take the point on her license, while I paid the fine. I agreed and transferred the money for the fine — and that was that. Now, a year later, we’ve both moved out and aren’t living together anymore. She recently messaged me saying her car insurance premium has gone up by $120/year, and it’s locked in that way for the next 3 years — all because she has that 1 demerit point. She’s now asking me to reimburse her for this ($360 total). I told her no — I appreciated her offer at the time, but I didn’t expect there to be more costs down the line. She offered to take the point, and I feel like it’s a bit unfair to come back a year later and try to charge me for ongoing consequences. She’s not happy, and now I’m wondering if I’m being unreasonable. AITA?
YTA Here in the UK, it's illegal to take points on your licence for someone else. Insurance always increases when you've been caught speeding, etc. If this thought didn't cross either of your minds, then you're both very niave. Pay her the money. Think of it this way, it's likely cheaper than what your insurance would go up by.

The carpool also includes Jenny (13F) (all fake names) who Maya used to be friends with, but the friendship ended after an argument over a group outfit. Since that happened, Maya is saying that she wants Jenny out of the carpool. Her explanation was that being around her ex-friend is giving her anxiety since she knows Jenny doesn’t like her anymore. I asked has Jenny been saying or doing anything to you? Maya said no, but she shouldn’t have to sit next to someone who doesn’t like her everyday. I need to back her up and stop driving Jenny in the carpool because I’m her sister and not Jenny’s I explained that Jenny’s mom obviously won’t participate in the carpool if Jenny isn’t allowed, which means Maya and Joey won’t have an affordable way to school on Mondays and Fridays anymore. I also told Maya that she can listen to her music or talk to our brother on the ride as a distraction. Maya kept arguing though and I was out of patience. So I told Maya that the fact is that not everybody’s gonna like you and broken friendships are going to happen in life. She needs to get over it and I better not hear about her trying to start any issues with Jenny or her mom over this. I’m conflicted because of my parents. Dad feels bad because broken friendships at that age are still painful, and he said I was mean for not offering to work out something. But Mom is out of patience as well because they’re already seated at opposite windows and Jenny isn’t saying or doing anything. She said she approved of what I told Maya because she’s not going to mess up the carpool and let them pay a ridiculous fee every semester just because she’s uncomfortable. Is Dad still right that AITA and should have tried to work something out with Maya?
NTA - the carpool is a financial necessity for your family. You have been considerate of your sister's feelings and given her the opportunity to let you know if Jenny is bullying her and been observant in the car. Your sister needs to suck it up and now is a good time to learn that. As for your parents, it is possible that you have now witnessed one of the reasons moms get mad at dads. Your dad is trying to be the "nice guy" regardless of your family's budget or actually helping your sister learn lessons in life (one of which is sometimes you have to work with people you don't like or who don't like you as long as everyone is civil). Your dad knows your mom won't put up with it and you have already put your foot down so he can look like the nice one who was overridden by mom.

NTA - your dad is being too soft here. Maya is 13, not 8, and she needs to learn that the world doesn't revolve around her comfort level. Jenny hasn't done anything to her, they just had a falling out. That's life. You're doing her a disservice by validating the idea that she can avoid people who hurt her feelings. That said, your mom is right - you already offered solutions (music, talking to Joey, sitting at opposite windows). Maya rejected all of them because what she really wants is for you to take her side and punish Jenny's family by kicking them out of the carpool. That's not fair to Jenny or her mom who are just trying to get their kid to school. Your parents are already paying for this carpool. You're the one driving. You set reasonable boundaries and Maya needs to deal with it. Broken friendships DO hurt at that age, but coddling her and removing Jenny from the carpool teaches her that avoidance is the solution instead of learning to be around people she's had conflict with - which is a skill she'll need for the rest of her life (work, college, etc). Stand your ground on this one. Your dad means well but he's enabling her to be inflexible and unreasonable.

the post (415 words)
So here is the backstory. In 2008 my parents lost most of their money and it got to the point they were no longer able to afford their house. My dad is quite a bit older than my mom, and his biggest worry is that he is not able to leave her enough to live and pay for a home/rent without having to financially rely on me and my sister. So this is the plan we came up with. We found a house with a large lot that we bought under my name. My parents put down a significant amount of their savings for the down payment for the property, and to build a detached Accessory Dwelling Unit for them to live in. I lived in the main house for a while, and my parents in the ADU. The agreement was once I moved out, we would rent out the main house. I would be responsible for the main house, maintenance, repairs, etc. and my parents are responsible for the ADU. We would split any property taxes not covered by the rental of the other property. I live in a very expensive area, so my parents will have a place to live for the rest of their lives with no stress about becoming homeless. And I end up with a great investment property that I already have quite a bit of equity in. I have the house in a trust. In the event of my untimely death, the ownership of the house will transfer to my parents, and then transfer to my partner/offsprings once they pass away. My wife wants me to change the trust to have the deed of the property go to her instead of my parents. She says she of course will let them live there but wants control of the property if I die. I feel really uncomfortable signing over the house my parents paid for to her. Even though it’s in my name and I’m spending some money on property taxes and maintenance, I consider it their house since they have paid for everything. Part of the reason I feel uncomfortable is because when we were updating our life insurance policies once we got married, she made sure I removed my parents as beneficiaries and added her on as 100% beneficiary. But then refused to add me as a beneficiary to her policy and had 100% going to her sister. AITA for refusing to sign the house over to her?
NTA. First a question - if you die and your parents inherit it, can't they leave it to whomever they want? Why would your will still apply at that point? Anyway, she seems to want everything in her name, but is not giving you anything in return. This sounds really wrong to me........ A will is a living document - it should be updated as your circumstances change - if you have kids, or your parents pass before you, presumably new arrangements will be needed. Right now, I can't see why you would change this - as you say, your parents put their money into too.

NTA. Your wife is being incredibly selfish here, and frankly, her behavior is a red flag. Let's break this down - your parents sacrificed their savings to build themselves a home on this property. They did this because your dad was worried about your mom's future security. This was a safety net for them. You agreed to this arrangement to help them, and it's worked out well for everyone - they have stability, you have an investment. Now your wife wants to take control of it? And she's already shown you exactly what she thinks about fairness - she made you remove your parents as life insurance beneficiaries and add her as 100%, but refused to do the same for you. That's not a partnership, that's someone looking out for themselves. If something happens to you, your wife would control the property and your parents would be living there at her discretion. That's not security for them - that's putting their home in the hands of someone who has already demonstrated she doesn't think about their wellbeing. What happens if she remarries? What if she decides to sell? Your parents could lose everything. Don't do this. The house stays in the trust as planned. If your wife can't accept that your parents' security is non-negotiable, that's a much bigger problem than this one property. You need to have a serious conversation about what marriage means to her, because right now it looks like it means taking as much as she can get.
